Global well-posedness of strong solutions to a bulk-surface Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model with non-degenerate mobilities in two dimensions
Jonas Stange

Abstract
. We examine a thermodynamically consistent diffuse interface model for bulk-surface viscous fluid mixtures. This model consists of a Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model in the bulk coupled to a surface Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard system on the boundary. In this paper, we address the global well-posedness of strong solutions in the two-dimensional setting, also covering the physically meaningful case of non-degenerate mobility functions. Lastly, we prove the uniqueness of the corresponding strong solutions and their continuous dependence on the initial data. Our approach hinges upon new well-posedness and regularity theory for a convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard equation with non-degenerate mobilities, as well as a bulk-surface Stokes equation with non-constant coefficients.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification

Primary: 76T06; Secondary:
35Q35, 76D03, 76D05, 76D07,

1.
Introduction

In this contribution, we study a thermodynamically consistent, diffuse interface model for bulk-surface viscous fluid mixtures with different densities in two space dimensions. The model was recently derived by Knopf and the author in
[
30
]
by means of local mass balance laws, local energy dissipation laws, and the Lagrange multiplier approach. The corresponding bulk-surface Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model reads as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}(\mathbf{v}\otimes(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{J}))=\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{T},

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{v}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q,

(1.1a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w}\otimes(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{K}))=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}+\mathbf{Z},

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{w}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma,

(1.1b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{v}|_{\Gamma},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma,

(1.1c)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi+\textnormal{div}(\phi\mathbf{v})=\textnormal{div}(m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q,

(1.1d)

\displaystyle\mu=-\Delta\phi+F^{\prime}(\phi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q,

(1.1e)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\psi\mathbf{w})=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta)-\beta m_{\Omega}(\phi)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma,

(1.1f)

\displaystyle\theta=-\Delta_{\Gamma}\psi+G^{\prime}(\psi)+\alpha\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma,

(1.1g)

\displaystyle K\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi=\alpha\psi-\phi,\hskip 20.00003ptLm_{\Omega}(\phi)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu=\beta\theta-\mu,

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003ptK,L\in[0,\infty]

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma,

(1.1h)

accompanied by the initial conditions

\displaystyle\phi|_{t=0}=\phi_{0},

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{v}|_{t=0}=\mathbf{v}_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}\Omega,

(1.1i)

\displaystyle\psi|_{t=0}=\psi_{0},

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{w}|_{t=0}=\mathbf{w}_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Gamma.

(1.1j)

In (
1.1
),
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is bounded domain with boundary
\Gamma\coloneqq\partial\Omega
. For brevity, we use the notation
Q=\Omega\times(0,\infty)
and
\Sigma=\Gamma\times(0,\infty)
. The outward pointing unit normal vector on
\Gamma
is denoted by
\mathbf{n}
, while
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}
denotes the outward normal derivative on the boundary. Moreover, the symbols
\nabla_{\Gamma}
,
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}
, and
\Delta_{\Gamma}
stand for the surface gradient, divergence, and the Laplace–Beltrami operator on
\Gamma
, respectively. We refer to Section
2.2
for a precise definition of these objects.

In the bulk domain
\Omega
, the motion of the mixture is described by the velocity field
\mathbf{v}:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, while on the boundary, the viscous dynamics are governed by the surface velocity field
\mathbf{w}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The location of the two materials within the bulk is captured by the phase-field
\phi:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, whereas the location of the fluids on the boundary is represented by the surface phase-field
\psi:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. The associated chemical potentials are given by
\mu:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
in the bulk and
\theta:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
on the surface.

The time evolution of the bulk and surface velocity fields,
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
, is governed by the bulk Navier–Stokes equation (
1.1a
) and the tangential surface Navier–Stokes equation (
1.1b
), respectively. In this framework, the two fields are coupled through the trace relation (
1.1c
)
1
, which directly links the motion of the bulk and surface materials. Both flows are assumed to be incompressible, which implies that the corresponding velocity fields are divergence-free. This boundary condition can be viewed as a generalization of the generalized Navier boundary condition (GNBC), which relates tangential stresses to slip velocities and uncompensated Young stresses. The GNBC plays a key role in modeling contact line phenomena, see, e.g.,
[
12
,
35
,
36
]
. The inclusion of additional viscous effects in system (
1.1
) is inspired by the classical fluid-mosaic model of membrane structure introduced in
[
37
]
. In that model, biological membranes are regarded as laterally incompressible, two-dimensional fluid layers with embedded proteins. This motivates treating the boundary as an active layer capable of supporting both lateral momentum transport and compositional dynamics. A rigorous continuum formulation of these ideas was later developed in
[
6
]
, where lipid bilayers are modeled by a surface Navier–Stokes equation with positive, but finite viscosity, which is expected to dominate relaxation dynamics. These findings and observations motivate the inclusion of a surface Navier–Stokes equation in system (
1.1
) to account for viscous and convective effects at the boundary.

In system (
1.1
),
\rho(\phi)
stands for the phase-field dependent density in the bulk, whereas
\sigma(\psi)
stands for the phase-field dependent density on the surface. They are given by

\displaystyle\rho(\phi)\coloneqq\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\phi+\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}+\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q,\hskip 20.00003pt\sigma(\psi)\coloneqq\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\psi+\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}+\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma.

Furthermore,
\mathbf{J}
and
\mathbf{K}
represent mass fluxes related to interfacial motion, and are given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}=-\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{K}=-\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma.

Moreover,
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}
are stress tensors, that are given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\,\mathbf{v}-p\mathbf{I}-\nabla\phi\otimes\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q,

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}-q\mathbf{I}-\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma.

Here, the phase-field dependent coefficients
\nu_{\Omega},\nu_{\Gamma}:[-1,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote the viscosity of the mixtures in the bulk and on the surface, respectively. A typical choice is the affine interpolation

\displaystyle\nu(s)=\frac{\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}}{2}s+\frac{\nu_{2}+\nu_{1}}{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for~}s\in[-1,1],

where
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are the constant viscosities of the two underlying fluids. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}\,\mathbf{v}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\big((\nabla\mathbf{v})+(\nabla\mathbf{v})^{\top}\big),\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\big((\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})^{\top}\big)

denote the bulk and surface symmetric gradient of
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
, respectively.
The vector
\mathbf{Z}
represents a surface force density that accounts for mechanical interactions between the bulk and the surface materials. It is of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}=-2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)[\mathbf{D}\,\mathbf{v}\mathbf{n}]_{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\alpha\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi-\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma.

(1.2)

The coefficient
\gamma:[-1,1]^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denotes a slip coefficient associated with tangential friction, which is allowed to depend on both the phase-fields. In (
1.2
),
\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}
represents a mass flux corresponding to the transfer of materials between bulk and surface, and is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}=-\beta\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma.

The time evolution of
\phi
and
\mu
is governed by the convective bulk Cahn–Hilliard subsystem (
1.1d
)-(
1.1e
), while the dynamics of
\psi
and
\theta
is described by the convective surface Cahn–Hilliard subsystem (
1.1f
)-(
1.1g
). These two subsystems are coupled through terms involving the normal derivatives
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi
and
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu
. Moreover, the phase-fields
\phi
and
\psi
are directly linked by the boundary condition (
1.1h
)
1
, which has to be understood in the following sense

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\phi=\alpha\psi&\text{if~}K=0,\\
K\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi=\alpha\psi-\phi&\text{if~}L\in(K,\infty),\\
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi=0&\text{if~}K=\infty,\end{cases}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma.

The boundary condition (
1.1h
)
2
, which couples the chemical potentials
\mu
and
\theta
has to be understood similarly. In these relations, the parameters
K,L\in[0,\infty]
distinguish different coupling regimes while
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
represent material-dependent physical effects, see, for instance,
[
19
,
28
]
for a more extensive description. Such coupling conditions belong to the class of dynamic boundary conditions, which generalize the classical homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi=\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu=0\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma,

commonly used in standard Cahn–Hilliard models. While Neumann boundary conditions remain popular for their simplicity, they can be too restrictive when accurate modeling of interfacial dynamics is required, see, e.g.,
[
19
,
28
]
. This limitation has motivated extensive research into dynamic boundary condition formulations. For a comprehensive overview, we refer to the survey
[
42
]
and the references therein. In particular, Cahn–Hilliard systems with dynamic boundary conditions that permit mass exchange between the bulk and the boundary have attracted significant interest in recent years, see, e.g.,
[
23
,
31
,
25
,
26
]
.

The functions
m_{\Omega},m_{\Gamma}:[-1,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are the so-called Onsager mobilities. They depend, in general, on the phase-field variables
\phi
and
\psi
, respectively. They model both the spatial locations and the intensity at which the underlying diffusion processes take place.

The functions
F^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
are the derivatives of double-well potentials
F
and
G
, respectively. A physically motivated example of such a double-well potential, in particular in applications related to materials science, is the logarithmic potential, which is also referred to as the Flory–Huggins potential. It is given as

\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{log}}(s)=\frac{\Theta}{2}\big[(1+s)\ln(1+s)+(1-s)\ln(1-s)\big]-\frac{\Theta_{c}}{2}s^{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for~}s\in[-1,1],

with the convention that
0\,\ln 0
is interpreted as zero. Here, we assume that
0<\Theta<\Theta_{c}
, where
\Theta
denotes the temperature of the mixture, and
\Theta_{c}
represents the critical temperature below which phase separation processes are to be expected. We point out that
W_{\mathrm{log}}
is classified as a singular potential, as it satisfies
W_{\mathrm{log}}^{\prime}(s)\rightarrow\pm\infty
as
s\rightarrow\pm 1
.

Employing the decompositions

\displaystyle-\textnormal{div}(\nabla\phi\otimes\nabla\phi)

\displaystyle=-\nabla\left(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}+F(\phi)\right)+\mu\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q,

\displaystyle-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi|^{2}+G(\psi)\right)+\theta\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi-\alpha\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma,

which readily follow from (
1.1e
) and (
1.1f
), respectively, we can replace the bulk and surface pressure
p
and
q
by

\displaystyle\overline{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq p+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}+F(\phi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q,

\displaystyle\overline{q}

\displaystyle\coloneqq q+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi|^{2}+G(\psi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma.

This allows us to rewrite (
1.1a
)
1
-(
1.1b
)
1
as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}(\mathbf{v}\otimes(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{J}))-\textnormal{div}(2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v})+\nabla\overline{p}=\mu\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q

(1.3a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w}\otimes(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{K})-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+\nabla_{\Gamma}\overline{q}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\theta\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi-2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\big[\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\,\mathbf{n}\big]_{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma.

(1.3b)

Moreover, for the subsequent analysis, it will prove useful to write (
1.3
) in the following so-called non-conservative form

\displaystyle\rho(\phi)\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}+\rho(\phi)(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}+(\mathbf{J}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}-\textnormal{div}(2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v})+\nabla\overline{p}=\mu\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q,

(1.4a)

\displaystyle\sigma(\psi)\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}+\sigma(\psi)(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w}+(\mathbf{K}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w}-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+\nabla_{\Gamma}\overline{q}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\theta\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi-2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\big[\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\,\mathbf{n}\big]_{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma.

(1.4b)

Lastly, multiplying (
1.1d
) with
\tfrac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}
and (
1.1f
) with
\tfrac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}
, we readily obtain that the time evolution of
\rho(\phi)
and
\sigma(\psi)
is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho(\phi)+\textnormal{div}(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=-\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{J}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q,

(1.5a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma(\psi)+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=-\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma,

(1.5b)

which will be useful later on in the analysis.

The energy functional associated with system (
1.1
) reads as

\displaystyle\begin{split}E_{\mathrm{tot}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi)&=\int_{\Omega}\frac{\rho(\phi)}{2}|\mathbf{v}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\sigma(\psi)}{2}|\mathbf{w}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}+F(\phi)\;\mathrm{d}x\\
&\quad+\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\psi|^{2}+G(\psi)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\chi(K)\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{2}(\alpha\psi-\phi)^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,\end{split}

(1.6)

where the function

\displaystyle\chi:[0,\infty]\rightarrow[0,\infty),\hskip 10.00002pt\chi(r)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{r},&\text{if~}r\in(0,\infty),\\
0,&\text{if~}r\in\{0,\infty\},\end{cases}

(1.7)

is used to distinguish different cases of the parameter
K
. Sufficiently regular solutions to (
1.1
) satisfy the following energy dissipation law

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}E_{\mathrm{tot}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi)+\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)|\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)|\mathbf{w}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad+\int_{\Omega}m_{\Omega}(\phi)|\nabla\mu|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}m_{\Gamma}(\psi)|\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\chi(L)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\theta-\mu)^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma=0\end{split}

(1.8)

on
[0,\infty)
, as well as the mass conservation law

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\beta\int_{\Omega}\phi(t)\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\psi(t)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma=\beta\int_{\Omega}\phi(0)\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\psi(0)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,&\textnormal{if~}L\in[0,\infty),\\
\int_{\Omega}\phi(t)\;\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}\phi(0)\;\mathrm{d}x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\int_{\Gamma}\psi(t)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma=\int_{\Gamma}\psi(0)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,&\textnormal{if~}L=\infty,\end{cases}

(1.9)

for all
t\in[0,\infty)
. This means that the mass is conserved separately in
\Omega
and on
\Gamma
in the case
L=\infty
, whereas, if
L\in[0,\infty)
, a transfer of material between bulk and surface is expected to occur, see, e.g.,
[
26
]
.

Related literature.

For related diffuse interface models without dynamic boundary conditions, such as the Abels–Garcke–Grün (AGG) model, the existence of strong solutions has been extensively investigated, see, for instance,
[
4
,
2
,
16
,
17
]
, as well as
[
22
,
21
]
. In contrast, for models with dynamic boundary conditions, the literature has so far focused primarily on the existence of weak solutions; we refer to
[
9
,
14
,
19
,
15
,
30
]
for representative results. Progress towards stronger notions of solutions has recently been made in
[
13
]
, where the authors established the existence of quasi-strong solutions for an Allen–Cahn–Navier–Stokes–Voigt system endowed with dynamic boundary conditions.

Goals and novelties of the paper.

System (
1.1
) was recently derived and analyzed in
[
30
]
, where the authors established the existence of weak solutions in the case of constant bulk and surface mobilities
m_{\Omega}
and
m_{\Gamma}
via a semi-Galerkin scheme.
The present work advances this analysis by proving the existence and uniqueness of strong solutions in the two-dimensional setting, allowing also for non-degenerate mobility functions.
Our approach is again based on a semi-Galerkin approximation, employing eigenfunctions of a bulk-surface Stokes operator to approximate the velocity fields. After constructing suitable approximate solutions by means of a fixed-point argument, we derive suitable uniform estimates. However, in contrast to the estimates performed in
[
30
]
for weak solutions, our proof is more involved, as we have to establish several higher-order estimates. For the convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard subsystem with non-degenerate mobilities, these estimates are based on the regularity theory developed in Appendix
A
, which extends the author’s recent work
[
39
]
on the non-convective case.
To obtain higher-order estimates for the velocity fields, we establish new regularity theory for a bulk-surface Stokes system with variable coefficients, presented in Appendix
B
, generalizing the framework developed in
[
30
, Section 5]
for the constant-coefficient case.
We further prove uniqueness and continuous dependence on the initial data of strong solutions for
L\in(0,\infty]
. The case
L=0
is excluded from our analysis, as the boundary condition (
1.1h
)
2
no longer allows reformulation of the normal derivative
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu
, making it unmanageable in our framework. Nevertheless, the situation
K=L=0
can be treated under additional structural assumptions on the potentials; see Section
3
.
Finally, we note that our results are restricted to the two-dimensional case. In three dimensions, the main difficulty lies in the loss of the separation property, which would necessitate more delicate approximation techniques to control the nonlinearities, similar to those employed in
[
17
]
for the AGG model. Furthermore, one cannot expect global strong solutions, but only local ones. The analysis can also be performed only for constant mobility functions, since for the convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard subsystem, the uniqueness of weak solutions and the existence of strong solutions are currently known only in this setting. The extension to the three-dimensional case will be addressed in future work.

Structure of this paper.
The remainder of this paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
, we present all the necessary mathematical notation and preliminaries. Then, in Section
3
, we state our main results regarding the existence of a unique global-in-time strong solution. Afterwards, Section
4
is devoted to the construction of a global-in-time strong solution. Lastly, in Section
5
, we establish the uniqueness of the corresponding strong solution provided that
L\in(0,\infty]
.

2.
Mathematical setting

2.1.
General notation and function spaces

We write
\mathbb{N}
to denote the set of natural numbers excluding zero, whereas
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. For any real Banach space
X
with
\|\cdot\|_{X}
, its dual space is denoted by
X^{\prime}
. The corresponding duality pairing of elements
\phi\in X^{\prime}
and
\zeta\in X
is denoted by
\langle\phi,\zeta\rangle_{X}
. If
X
is a Hilbert space, we write
(\cdot,\cdot)_{X}
to denote its inner product. Given
p\in[1,\infty]
and an interval
I\subset[0,\infty)
, the Bochner space
L^{p}(I,X)
consists of all Bochner measurable,
p
-integrable functions defined on
I
with values in
X
. Moreover, the space
W^{1,p}(I;X)
consists of all functions
f\in L^{p}(I;X)
such that their vector-valued distributional derivative satisfies
\partial_{t}f\in L^{p}(I;X)
. In particular, we set
H^{1}(I;X)=W^{1,2}(I;X)
. Furthermore,
L^{p}_{\mathrm{uloc}}(I;X)
denotes the space of functions
f\in L^{p}(I;X)
such that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\mathrm{uloc}}(I;X)}\coloneqq\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big(\int_{I\cap[t,t+1)}\|f(s)\|_{X}^{p}\;\mathrm{d}s\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty.

If
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is a finite interval, we simply have
L^{p}_{\mathrm{uloc}}(I;X)=L^{p}(I;X)
. Further, the set of continuous functions
f:I\rightarrow X
is denoted by
C(I;X)
. Moreover,
C_{w}(I;X)
denotes the space of functions
f:I\to X
which are continuous on
I
with respect to the weak topology on
X
, i.e., the map
I\ni t\mapsto\langle\phi,f(t)\rangle_{X}
is continuous for all
\phi\in X^{\prime}
.

In the remainder of this section, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d=2,3
, be a bounded domain with sufficiently smooth boundary
\Gamma\coloneqq\partial\Omega
, and let
\mathbf{n}
denote the exterior unit normal vector field on
\Gamma
. For any
s\geq 0
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, the Lebesgue and Sobolev–Slobodeckij spaces for functions mapping from
\Omega
to
\mathbb{R}
are denoted as
L^{p}(\Omega)
and
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
, respectively. We write
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}
and
\|\cdot\|_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}
to denote the standard norms on these spaces. If
p=2
, we use the notation
H^{s}(\Omega)=W^{s,2}(\Omega)
, with the convention that
H^{0}(\Omega)
is identified with
L^{2}(\Omega)
. For the Lebesgue and Sobolev–Slobodeckij spaces on the boundary
\Gamma
, we use an analogous notation. The corresponding spaces of vector-valued functions mapping into
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or matrix-valued functions mapping into
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are denoted by boldface letters, namely
\mathbf{L}^{p}
,
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}
, and
\mathbf{H}^{s}
. In particular, we write
\mathbf{L}^{p}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
,
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
and
\mathbf{H}^{s}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
to denote the corresponding spaces of tangential vector fields on
\Gamma
.

Moreover, we further introduce the first-order homogeneous Sobolev spaces

\displaystyle\dot{H}^{1}\Omega)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big\{u\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)\,:\,\nabla u\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)\big\}\big/_{\sim}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{H}^{1}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big\{v\in L^{2}(\Gamma)\,:\,\nabla_{\Gamma}v\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)\big\}\big/_{\sim}\,.

Here,
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
is the collection of all functions, which belong to
L^{2}(K)
for every compact subset
K\subset\Omega
. On the boundary, we simply consider
L^{2}(\Gamma)
, since
\Gamma
is itself a compact submanifold.
Moreover, the notation
/_{\sim}\,
indicates that functions differing only by an additive constant are considered equivalent and thus identified with each other. Consequently, the functionals
{\|\nabla\,\cdot\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}}
and
{\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\,\cdot\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}}
define norms on the spaces
\dot{H}^{1}(\Omega)
and
\dot{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
, respectively.

Furthermore, we write
\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}
to denote the orthogonal projection of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into the tangent space of the submanifold
\Gamma
.
This means that
\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}
is a linear map, which can be represented as

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}=\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{n},

where
\mathbf{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the unity matrix.
For any vector field
\mathbf{w}
on
\Gamma
, we also use the notation

\displaystyle[\mathbf{w}]_{\tau}=\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w}).

2.2.
Differential operators in the bulk and on the surface

Let
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
be scalar functions, let
\mathbf{v}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbf{w}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be vector fields, and let
\mathbf{A}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\mathbf{B}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be matrix-valued functions.
For now, we assume that the boundary
\Gamma
and all these functions are sufficiently regular.
As usual,
\nabla f
,
\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}
, and
\Delta f=\textnormal{div}(\nabla f)
denote the gradient of
f
, the divergence of
\mathbf{v}
and the Laplacian of
f
.
Analogously,
\nabla_{\Gamma}g
,
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}
, and
\Delta_{\Gamma}g=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\nabla_{\Gamma}g)
are the tangential gradient of
g
, the surface divergence of
\mathbf{w}
and the Laplace–Beltrami operator applied to
g
.
We point out that
\nabla f
and
\nabla_{\Gamma}g
are to be understood as column vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For bulk vector fields, the gradient is defined as

\displaystyle\nabla\mathbf{v}=\begin{pmatrix}(\nabla\mathbf{v}_{1})^{T}\\
\vdots\\
(\nabla\mathbf{v}_{d})^{T}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

where
\mathbf{v}_{1},...,\mathbf{v}_{d}
denote the components of
\mathbf{v}
. In other words,
\nabla\mathbf{v}
is the Jacobian of
\mathbf{v}
. On the surface
\Gamma
, the projective (covariant) gradient of
\mathbf{w}
is defined as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}\nabla\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma},

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}
is an extension of
\mathbf{w}
to an open neighborhood of
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. However, the expression
\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}
does not depend on the precise choice of this extension. If
\mathbf{w}
is a tangential vector field, its gradient can also be expressed as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}\begin{pmatrix}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{1})^{T}\\
\vdots\\
(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{d})^{T}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

where
\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{d}
denote the components of
\mathbf{w}
.
For the matrix-valued functions
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
, we further define the divergences

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{A}_{1\ast}\\
\vdots\\
\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{A}_{d\ast}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}\begin{pmatrix}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{B}_{1\ast}\\
\vdots\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{B}_{d\ast}\end{pmatrix}.

Here,
\mathbf{A}_{1\ast},...,\mathbf{A}_{d\ast}
and
\mathbf{B}_{1\ast},...,\mathbf{B}_{d\ast}
denote the rows of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
, respectively.

2.3.
Bulk-surface product spaces

For any real number
s\geq 0
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{p}\coloneqq L^{p}(\Omega)\times L^{p}(\Gamma),\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{W}^{s,p}\coloneqq W^{s,p}(\Omega)\times W^{s,p}(\Gamma).

We write
\mathcal{H}^{s}=\mathcal{W}^{s,2}
and identify
\mathcal{L}^{2}
with
\mathcal{H}^{s}
. Note that
\mathcal{H}^{s}
is a Hilbert space with respect to the inner product

\displaystyle\big((\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\big)_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\coloneqq(\phi,\zeta)_{H^{s}(\Omega)}+(\psi,\xi)_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}(\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{s},

and its induced norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\coloneqq(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}^{\frac{1}{2}}
. We further recall that the duality pairing on
\mathcal{H}^{1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\big<(\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}=(\phi,\zeta)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+(\psi,\xi)_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}

for all
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
if
(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
.

For every
\phi\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\prime}
, we denote by
{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}=|\Omega|^{-1}\langle\phi,1\rangle_{H^{1}(\Omega)}
its generalized mean value over
\Omega
. If
\phi\in L^{1}(\Omega)
, its spatial mean can simply be expressed as
{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}=|\Omega|^{-1}\int_{\Omega}\phi\;\mathrm{d}x
. The spatial mean for a function
\psi
on
\Gamma
, denoted by
{\langle\psi\rangle}_{\Gamma}
, is defined similarly. We further define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}\coloneqq L^{2}_{(0)}(\Omega)\times L^{2}_{(0)}(\Gamma),

where

\displaystyle L^{2}_{(0)}(\Omega)\coloneqq\big\{\phi\in L^{2}(\Omega):{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}=0\big\},\hskip 20.00003ptL^{2}_{(0)}(\Gamma)\coloneqq\big\{\psi\in L^{2}(\Gamma):{\langle\psi\rangle}_{\Gamma}=0\big\}.

Now, let
L\in[0,\infty]
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. We introduce the subspaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathcal{H}^{1},&\text{if~}L\in(0,\infty],\\
\big\{(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}:\phi=\beta\psi\text{~a.e. on~}\Gamma\big\},&\text{if~}L=0,\end{cases}

endowed with the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}\coloneqq(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}
and its induced norm. The space
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}
is a Hilbert space. Moreover, we define the product

\displaystyle\big<(\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}=(\phi,\zeta)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+(\psi,\xi)_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}

for all
(\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
. By means of the Riesz representation theorem, this product can be extended to a duality pairing on
(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}\times\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}
, which will also be denoted as
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}
.

Next, for
(\phi,\psi)\in(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}
, we defined the generalized bulk-surface mean

\displaystyle\textnormal{mean}(\phi,\psi)\coloneqq\frac{\big<(\phi,\psi),(\beta,1)\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}}{\beta^{2}|\Omega|+|\Gamma|},

which reduces to

\displaystyle\textnormal{mean}(\phi,\psi)=\frac{\beta|\Omega|{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}+|\Gamma|{\langle\psi\rangle}_{\Gamma}}{\beta^{2}|\Omega|+|\Gamma|}

if
(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
. We then define the closed linear subspaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}=\begin{cases}\{(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}:\textnormal{mean}(\phi,\psi)=0\},&\text{if~}L\in[0,\infty),\\
\{(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}:{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}={\langle\psi\rangle}_{\Gamma}=0\},&\text{if~}L=\infty.\end{cases}

Note that these subspaces are Hilbert spaces with respect to the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}
. We further introduce the bilinear form

\displaystyle\big((\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\big)_{L,\beta}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla\zeta\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\chi(L)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\psi-\phi)(\beta\xi-\zeta)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

for all
(\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
, where

\displaystyle\chi:[0,\infty]\rightarrow[0,\infty),\hskip 20.00003pt\chi(r)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{r},&\text{if~}r\in(0,\infty),\\
0,&\text{if~}r\in\{0,\infty\}.\end{cases}

Moreover, we set

\displaystyle\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{L,\beta}\coloneqq\big((\phi,\psi),(\phi,\psi)\big)_{L,\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}

for all
(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
. The bilinear form defines and inner product on
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}
, and
\|\cdot\|_{L,\beta}
defines a norm on
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}
, that is equivalent to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}
, see, e.g.,
[
27
, Corollary A.2]
. The space
(\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta},(\cdot,\cdot)_{L,\beta},\|\cdot\|_{L,\beta})
is a Hilbert space. Then, we define the spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{(\phi,\psi)\in(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}:\textnormal{mean}(\phi,\psi)=0\},&\text{if~}L\in[0,\infty),\\
\{(\phi,\psi)\in(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\prime}:{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}={\langle\psi\rangle}_{\Gamma}=0\},&\text{if~}L=\infty.\end{cases}

2.4.
Spaces of tangential and divergence-free vector fields

We introduce the spaces

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)\coloneqq\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega):\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}=0\ \text{in~}\Omega,\ \mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0\ \text{on~}\Gamma\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)\coloneqq\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma):\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}=0,\ \mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0\ \text{on~}\Gamma\},

and we set

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}\coloneqq\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)\times\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma).

Then, for
s\geq 0
and
p\in[2,\infty]
, we define
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)=\mathbf{W}^{s,p}(\Omega)\cap\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)
. Analogously, we define
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)=\mathbf{W}^{s,p}(\Gamma)\cap\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)
, and then set
\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}=\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)\times\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)
. Furthermore, for
s>\frac{1}{2}
, we set

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}:\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0,\ \mathbf{v}|_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{w}\ \text{on~}\Gamma\},

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}_{0,\textnormal{div}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}_{0}\cap\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}.

As before, we use the notation
\mathbfcal{H}^{s}_{0}=\mathbfcal{W}^{s,2}_{0}
as well as
\mathbfcal{H}^{s}_{0,\textnormal{div}}=\mathbfcal{W}^{s,2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
.

2.5.
Important tools.

Throughout this paper, we will frequently use the following bulk-surface Poincaré inequality, which has been established in
[
27
, Lemma A.1]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
K\in[0,\infty)
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\alpha\beta|\Omega|+|\Gamma|\neq 0
. Then there exists a constant
C_{P}>0
depending only on
K,\alpha,\beta
and
\Omega
such that

\displaystyle\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq C_{P}\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{K,\alpha}

(2.1)

for all pairs
(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha}
satisfying
\textnormal{mean}(\phi,\psi)=0
.

Furthermore, we recall the following trace interpolation inequality (see, e.g.,
[
34
]
):

Lemma 2.2
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq C\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}u\in H^{1}(\Omega).

(2.2)

Next, we recall the following interpolation inequality:

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with sufficiently regular boundary
\Gamma
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
, such that it holds that

\displaystyle\|(\zeta,\xi)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}\leq C\|(\zeta,\xi)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|(\zeta,\xi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}.

(2.3)

Note that, as
\Gamma
is
1
-dimensional submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, this bulk-surface inequality readily follows directly from the classical Ladyzhenskaya interpolation inequality applied in the bulk and on the surface, respectively.

Lastly, we present a bulk-surface Korn-type inequality, which has been recently established in
[
30
, Lemma 5.1]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

There exists a constant
C_{K}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}}\leq C_{K}\big(\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(2.4)

for all
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
.

In particular, as a consequence of Lemma
2.4
, the norm

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}\coloneqq\big(\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|(\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

is equivalent to the standard
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}}
norm on
\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
.

2.6.
Elliptic problems with bulk-surface interaction.

We end this section by presenting results for two different elliptic equations with bulk-surface interaction. The results are extracted from
[
27
]
and
[
39
]
, respectively. We refer to these works for a more comprehensive exposition.

We start with the following problem

\displaystyle-\Delta u

\displaystyle=f

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}\Omega,

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\Gamma}v+\beta\partial_{\mathbf{n}}u

\displaystyle=g

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Gamma,

(2.5)

\displaystyle L\partial_{\mathbf{n}}u

\displaystyle=\beta v-u

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Gamma,

which has been studied in
[
27
]
. One can show that, employing the Lax–Milgram theorem in combination with Lemma
2.1
, for any
(f,g)\in\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}
, there exists a unique weak solution
(u,v)\in\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}
to (
2.6
) in the sense that it satisfies the weak formulation

\displaystyle\big((u,v),(\zeta,\xi))_{L,\beta}=\big\langle(f,g),(\zeta,\xi)\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}

for all
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}
, where the bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{L,\beta}
on
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}\times\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}
is defined as

\displaystyle\big((u,v),(\zeta,\xi)\big)_{L,\beta}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\zeta\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}v\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\chi(L)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta v-u)(\beta\xi-\zeta)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

Consequently, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on the parameters of the system, such that

\displaystyle\|(u,v)\|_{L,\beta}\leq C\|(f,g)\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}}

for all
(f,g)\in\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}
. This allows us to define a solution operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}:\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{1},\hskip 20.00003pt(f,g)\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(f,g)=\big(\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}(f,g),\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Gamma}(f,g)\big)

in the sense that, for given
(f,g)\in\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}
,
\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(f,g)
is the unique weak solution to (
2.6
). Furthermore, we can define an inner product and its induced norm on
\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}
via

\displaystyle\big((f,g),(\zeta,\xi)\big)_{L,\beta,\ast}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(f,g),\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(\zeta,\xi)\big)_{L,\beta},

\displaystyle\|(f,g)\|_{L,\beta,\ast}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big((f,g),(f,g)\big)_{L,\beta,\ast}^{\frac{1}{2}}

for all
(f,g),(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}
. This norm is equivalent to the standard norm
\|\cdot\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}}
on
\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}
.

Next, let
m_{\Omega},m_{\Gamma}\in C([-1,1])
be such that there exist two constants
m_{\ast},m^{\ast}>0
with

\displaystyle 0<m_{\ast}\leq m_{\Omega}(s),m_{\Gamma}(s)\leq m^{\ast}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}s\in[-1,1].

(2.6)

Then, given two measurable functions
\phi:\Omega\rightarrow[-1,1]
and
\psi:\Gamma\rightarrow[-1,1]
, we introduce the following elliptic problem

\displaystyle-\textnormal{div}(m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla u)

\displaystyle=f

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}\Omega,

\displaystyle-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\nabla_{\Gamma}v)+\beta m_{\Omega}(\phi)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}u

\displaystyle=g

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Gamma,

(2.7)

\displaystyle Lm_{\Omega}(\phi)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}u

\displaystyle=\beta v-u

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Gamma,

which has been recently studied in
[
39
, Section 4]
. The system (
2.6
) can be seen as a generalization of (
2.6
).
Similarly to the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}
, we can introduce a solution operator
\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi]:\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{1}
in the following way: for any
(f,g)\in\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}
,
\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](f,g)
is the unique function satisfying

\displaystyle\big((u,v),(\zeta,\xi)\big)_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi]}=\big\langle(f,g),(\zeta,\xi)\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}.

(2.8)

Here, we have used the notation

\displaystyle\big((u,v),(\zeta,\xi)\big)_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi]}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla u\cdot\nabla\zeta\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\nabla_{\Gamma}v\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\chi(L)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta v-u)(\beta\xi-\zeta)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

Note that this bilinear form defines an inner product on
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}
. The corresponding norm on
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}
is then defined as

\displaystyle\|(f,g)\|_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi]}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big((f,g),(f,g)\big)_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi]}^{\frac{1}{2}}

for all
(f,g)\in\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}
. We readily see that there exists a constant
C>0
, that only depends on the parameters of the system, such that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\|_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi]}\leq C\|(f,g)\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}}

(2.9)

for all
(f,g)\in\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}
. Moreover, on account of (
2.6
), it holds that

\displaystyle\min\{1,\sqrt{m_{\ast}}\}\|(f,g)\|_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi]}\leq\|(f,g)\|_{L,\beta}\leq\max\{1,\sqrt{m^{\ast}}\}\|(f,g)\|_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi]}

(2.10)

for all
(f,g)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}
. In particular, the norms
\|\cdot\|_{L,\beta}
and
\|\cdot\|_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi]}
are equivalent on
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}
.

Next, we define an inner product and its induced norm on
\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}
by

\displaystyle\big((f,g),(\zeta,\xi)\big)_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi],\ast}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](f,g),\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](\zeta,\xi)\big)_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi]},

\displaystyle\|(f,g)\|_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi],\ast}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big((f,g),(f,g)\big)_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi],\ast}^{\frac{1}{2}}

for all
(f,g),(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}
. Using the respective weak formulation satisfied by the solution operators
\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}
and
\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi]
, we deduce that the norms
\|\cdot\|_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi],\ast}
and
\|\cdot\|_{L,\beta,\ast}
are equivalent on
\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}
with

\displaystyle\min\{1,\sqrt{m_{\ast}}\}\|(f,g)\|_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi],\ast}\leq\|(f,g)\|_{L,\beta,\ast}\leq\max\{1,\sqrt{m^{\ast}}\}\|(f,g)\|_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi],\ast}

(2.11)

for all
(f,g)\in\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}
. In particular, we also obtain that
\|\cdot\|_{L,\beta,[\phi,\psi],\ast}
and
\|\cdot\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}}
are equivalent on
\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}
. Besides, in view of the weak formulation (
2.8
) and the bulk-surface Poincaré inequality (
2.1
), we have

\displaystyle\|(f,g)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq\max\{1,\sqrt{m^{\ast}}\}C_{P}C\|\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\|_{L,\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|(f,g)\|_{K,\alpha}^{\frac{1}{2}}

(2.12)

for all
(f,g)\in\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha}
and
K\in[0,\infty)
.

Now, we recall some elliptic estimates related to the problem (
2.6
) for
d=2
, whose proofs can be found in
[
39
, Section 4]
. To this end, we additionally assume that
m_{\Omega},m_{\Gamma}\in C^{1}([-1,1])
as well as
(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{W}^{2,4}
. Let
(f,g)\in\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}\cap\mathcal{L}^{p}
for some
2\leq p<\infty.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\|_{\mathcal{W}^{2,p}}\leq C\big(\|(\nabla\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\cdot\nabla\phi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Gamma}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}+\|(f,g)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\big).

(2.13)

In particular, taking
p=2
in (
2.13
), and by using (
2.3
), we find

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|(\nabla\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\cdot\nabla\phi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Gamma}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}+\|(f,g)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|(\nabla\phi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{4}}\|(\nabla\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}[\phi,\psi](f,g),\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Gamma}[\phi,\psi](f,g))\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{4}}+\|(f,g)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|(\nabla\phi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\|_{L,\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\|(f,g)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\big).

Consequently, we infer from Young’s inequality that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}\leq C\big(\|(\nabla\phi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}}\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}\|\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\|_{L,\beta}+\|(f,g)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\big).

(2.14)

Lastly, if
(f,g)\in\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}
and
m_{\Omega},m_{\Gamma}\in C^{2}([-1,1])
, then there exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}[\phi,\psi](f,g)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq C\big(1+\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}(L)\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\times\Bigg(\Big\|\bigg(\frac{f}{m_{\Omega}(\phi)},\frac{g}{m_{\Gamma}(\psi)}\bigg)\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}

(2.15)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\Big\|\bigg(\frac{m_{\Omega}^{\prime}(\phi)\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}[\phi,\psi](f,g)}{m_{\Omega}(\phi)},\frac{m_{\Gamma}^{\prime}(\psi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Gamma}[\phi,\psi](f,g)}{m_{\Gamma}(\psi)}\bigg)\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\Bigg),

where
\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}
denotes the indicator function of the set
\{0\}
.

2.7.
Assumptions

Now, we fix some assumptions that are supposed to hold throughout the remainder of this paper.

(A1)

We consider a bounded domain
\emptyset\neq\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
C^{3}
boundary
\Gamma\coloneqq\partial\Omega
. We further use the notation

\displaystyle Q\coloneqq\Omega\times(0,\infty),\hskip 10.00002pt\Sigma\coloneqq\Gamma\times(0,\infty).

(A2)

The constants occurring in the system (
1.1
) satisfy
\alpha\in[-1,1]
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\alpha\beta|\Omega|+|\Gamma|\neq 0
.

(A3)

The density functions
\rho
and
\sigma
are given by

\displaystyle\rho(s)=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}s+\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{1}+\tilde{\rho}_{2}}{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma(s)=\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}s+\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{1}+\tilde{\sigma}_{2}}{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}s\in[-1,1].

Here,
\tilde{\rho}_{1},\tilde{\rho}_{2}>0
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{1},\tilde{\sigma}_{2}>0
are the specific densities of the two fluid components in the bulk and on the surface, respectively. Moreover, we use the notation

\displaystyle\rho_{\ast}\coloneqq\min\{\tilde{\rho}_{1},\tilde{\rho}_{2}\},\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\rho^{\ast}\coloneqq\max\{\tilde{\rho}_{1},\tilde{\rho}_{2}\},

\displaystyle\sigma_{\ast}\coloneqq\min\{\tilde{\sigma}_{1},\tilde{\sigma}_{2}\},\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\sigma^{\ast}\coloneqq\max\{\tilde{\sigma}_{1},\tilde{\sigma}_{2}\},

By this definition, we clearly have

\displaystyle 0<\rho_{\ast}\leq\rho(s)\leq\rho^{\ast}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt0<\sigma_{\ast}\leq\sigma(s)\leq\sigma^{\ast}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}s\in[-1,1].

(A4)

For the coefficients we assume
m_{\Omega},m_{\Gamma}\in C^{2}([-1,1])
,
\nu_{\Omega},\nu_{\Gamma}\in C^{2}([-1,1])
,
\gamma\in C^{0,1}([-1,1]^{2})
. Moreover, we postulate the existence of positive constants
m_{\ast},m^{\ast},\nu_{\ast},\nu^{\ast},\gamma_{\ast},\gamma^{\ast}
such that for all
s\in[-1,1]
,

\displaystyle 0<m_{\ast}\leq m_{\Omega}(s),m_{\Gamma}(s)\leq m^{\ast}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt0<\nu_{\ast}\leq\nu_{\Omega}(s),\nu_{\Gamma}(s)\leq\nu^{\ast}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}s\in[-1,1],

(2.16)

and

\displaystyle 0<\gamma_{\ast}\leq\gamma(s,r)\leq\gamma^{\ast}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}(s,r)\in[-1,1]^{2}.

(A5)

The bulk free energy density is given by

\displaystyle F(s)=F_{0}(s)-\frac{c_{F}}{2}s^{2}

for some
c_{F}\in\mathbb{R}
, satisfying
F_{0}\in C([-1,1])\cap C^{3}(-1,1)
. We assume that

\displaystyle\lim_{s\searrow-1}F_{0}^{\prime}(s)=-\infty\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and~}\hskip 10.00002pt\lim_{s\nearrow 1}F_{0}^{\prime}(s)=+\infty,

and the existence of a constant
\Theta>0
such that

\displaystyle F_{0}^{\prime\prime}(s)\geq\Theta\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $s\in(-1,1)$.}

(2.17)

As usual we extend
F_{0}(s)=+\infty
for any
s\not\in[-1,1]
. Without loss of generality, we assume
F_{0}(0)=F_{0}^{\prime}(0)=0
. In particular, we thus have
F_{0}(s)\geq 0
for all
s\in[-1,1]
due to the strong convexity of
F_{0}
.
Analogously, the free energy density on the boundary is given by

\displaystyle G(s)=G_{0}(s)-\frac{c_{G}}{2}s^{2}

for some
c_{G}\in\mathbb{R}
and
G_{0}\in C([-1,1])\cap C^{3}(-1,1)
. We assume that
G_{0}
satisfies analogous assumptions to
F_{0}
.

(A6)

There exist constants
\kappa_{1}>0
and
\kappa_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle|F_{0}^{\prime}(\alpha s)|\leq\kappa_{1}|G_{0}^{\prime}(s)|+\kappa_{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}s\in(-1,1).

(A7)

We assume that one of the following conditions hold:

(A.7.1)

There exist constant
C_{\sharp}>0
and
\gamma_{\sharp}\in[1,2)
such that

\displaystyle F_{0}^{\prime\prime}(s)\leq C_{\sharp}\mathbf{e}^{C_{\sharp}|F_{0}^{\prime}(s)|^{\gamma_{\sharp}}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}s\in(-1,1).

(A.7.2)

As
\delta\searrow 0
, for some
\kappa>\frac{1}{2}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{F_{0}^{\prime}(1-2\delta)}=O\bigg(\frac{1}{|\ln\delta|^{\kappa}}\bigg),\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{1}{|F_{0}^{\prime}(-1+2\delta)|}=O\bigg(\frac{1}{|\ln\delta|^{\kappa}}\bigg).

3.
Main results

In this section, we formulate the main results of this paper. Our first main result is concerned with the existence of global strong solutions to system (
1.1
).

Theorem 3.1
.

Let the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A7)
hold, and let
K\in(0,\infty)
and
L\in[0,\infty]
. Assume that
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{\textnormal{div}}
, and let
(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
be such that

\displaystyle\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 1\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\leq 1.

(3.1a)

Furthermore, we assume that

\displaystyle\beta\textnormal{mean}(\phi_{0},\psi_{0}),\ \textnormal{mean}(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})\in(-1,1),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{if~}L\in[0,\infty),

(3.1b)

and

\displaystyle{\langle\phi_{0}\rangle}_{\Omega},\ {\langle\psi_{0}\rangle}_{\Gamma}\in(-1,1),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{if~}L=\infty.

(3.1c)

We further assume that the following compatibility assumption holds:

(C)

There exists
(\mu_{0},\theta_{0})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}
such that for all
(\eta,\vartheta)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
it holds

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Omega}\mu_{0}\eta\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\theta_{0}\vartheta\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla\eta+F^{\prime}(\phi_{0})\eta\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\vartheta+G^{\prime}(\psi_{0})\vartheta\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\quad+\chi(K)\int_{\Gamma}(\alpha\psi_{0}-\phi_{0})(\alpha\vartheta-\eta)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.\end{aligned}

Moreover, in the case
L=0
, we suppose that the following compatibility assumption on the densities

\displaystyle\beta(\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1})=\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}

(3.2)

holds.
Then, there exists a global strong solution
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\mu,\theta)
to (
1.1
) satisfying the regularities

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle\in BC([0,\infty);\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}})\cap L^{2}_{\mathrm{uloc}}([0,\infty);\mathbfcal{H}^{2})\cap H^{1}_{\mathrm{uloc}}(0,\infty;\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}),

(3.3)

\displaystyle(p,q)

\displaystyle\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{uloc}}(0,\infty;\mathcal{H}^{1}\cap\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}),

(3.4)

\displaystyle(\phi,\psi)

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(0,\infty;\mathcal{H}^{3}),\hskip 20.00003pt(\partial_{t}\phi,\partial_{t}\psi)\in L^{\infty}(0,\infty;(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime})\cap L^{2}_{\mathrm{uloc}}(0,\infty;\mathcal{H}^{1}),

(3.5)

\displaystyle(\mu,\theta)

\displaystyle\in BC([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})\cap L^{2}_{\mathrm{uloc}}([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}^{3})\cap H^{1}_{\mathrm{uloc}}(0,\infty;(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\prime}),

(3.6)

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}(\phi

\displaystyle),G^{\prime}(\psi)),\ (F^{\prime\prime}(\phi),G^{\prime\prime}(\psi))\in L^{\infty}(0,\infty;\mathcal{L}^{p})

(3.7)

for any
2\leq p<\infty
, in the sense that the equations (
1.1a
), (
1.1d
) and (
1.1e
) are satisfied a.e. on
\Omega\times(0,\infty)
, while the equations (
1.1b
) and (
1.1f
)-(
1.1g
) and the boundary conditions (
1.1c
) and (
1.1h
) are satisfied a.e. on
\Gamma\times(0,\infty)
. Moreover, the solution is such that
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})|_{t=0}=(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
a.e. in
\Omega\times\Gamma
and
(\phi,\psi)|_{t=0}=(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})
a.e. in
\Omega\times\Gamma
.

Remark 3.2
.

The case
K=0
is not admissible in the setting of non-degenerate mobility functions. This issue appears in the analysis of the well-posedness of weak solutions to the convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard subsystem (
A.1
), as discussed in Appendix
A
(see also
[
39
]
). In fact, only for
K\in(0,\infty)
does the corresponding weak solution
(\phi,\psi,\mu,\theta)
satisfy the enhanced regularity
(\phi,\psi)\in L^{4}_{\mathrm{uloc}}([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}^{2})
(see
[
20
, Theorem 3.3]
), which is a crucial step in the proof of uniqueness of weak solutions (see also
[
11
]
). If
K=0
, one only obtains
(\phi,\psi)\in L^{3}_{\mathrm{uloc}}([0,\infty)\mathcal{H}^{2})
, which appears insufficient to complete the argument.
By contrast, in the case of constant mobilities, say
m_{\Omega}=m_{\Gamma}\equiv 1
, the choice
K=0
becomes admissible since the aforementioned higher-order norms are not required in this setting. Indeed, for constant mobilities, one can establish the existence of a strong solution with the additional regularity

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(0,\infty;\mathbfcal{H}^{2})\cap H^{1}(0,\infty;\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}),

\displaystyle(p,q)

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(0,\infty;\mathcal{H}^{1}\cap\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}),

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\phi,\partial_{t}\psi)

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(0,\infty;\mathcal{H}^{1}),

\displaystyle(\nabla\mu,\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta)

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(0,\infty;\mathbfcal{H}^{2}),\hskip 20.00003pt(\mu,\theta)\in H^{1}(0,\infty;(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha})^{\prime}).

These enhanced regularities can be achieved by using the regularity theory for the convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard equation with constant mobility functions as developed in
[
29
]
and
[
20
]
.

Remark 3.3
.

We comment on the compatibility condition (
3.2
), which was needed in our analysis in the case
L=0
. The compatibility condition (
3.2
) ensures that, formally,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}=\Big(\beta\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}-\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\Big)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu\,\mathbf{w}=0\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on }\Gamma,

(3.8)

which arises as a term on the right-hand side of the non-conservative form (
1.4b
). This assumption differs from the condition used in
[
30
, Theorem 4.2]
to guarantee the existence of weak solutions.
Namely, in
[
30
]
, it was assumed that

\displaystyle\beta(\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1})=-(\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}),

(3.9)

which implies, formally,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}=\Big(\beta\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}+\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\Big)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu\,\mathbf{w}=0\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on }\Gamma,

(3.10)

which instead appears on the right-hand side of (
1.3b
). In the analysis of
[
30
]
, the weak formulation of (
1.3
) is employed, so the condition (
3.9
) is necessary to eliminate (
3.10
), as otherwise this term couldn’t be handled. In contrast, to prove Theorem
3.1
we rely on the non-conservative formulation (
1.4
), which allows us to derive suitable bounds for the weak time derivative of the velocity fields. Since we could not find a way to control the boundary contribution involving
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu
from the fluxes
\mathbf{J}
and
\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}
, we imposed (
3.2
), which ensures that these terms vanish, see (
3.8
). This explains why the two compatibility conditions (
3.2
) and (
3.9
) differ by a minus sign. Note that these conditions are only required in the case
L=0
, since when
L\in(0,\infty]
, the boundary condition (
1.1h
)
2
allows us to express the normal derivative
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu
as
\tfrac{1}{Lm_{\Omega}(\phi)}(\beta\theta-\mu)
.

Our next main result is concerned with the uniqueness of strong solutions to (
1.1
)

Theorem 3.4
.

Let the assumptions from Theorem
3.1
hold. Additionally, assume that
L\in(0,\infty]
. Then, the global strong solution to (
1.1
) is unique.

Remark 3.5
.

We remark on the assumption
L\in(0,\infty]
imposed in Theorem
3.4
. This limitation again stems from the boundary condition (
1.1h
)
2
: when
L=0
, it is not possible to reformulate the normal derivative
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu
, and we cannot control this term otherwise. Thus, our analysis does not extend to the case
L=0
. This difficulty already appears in the bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard system, even without coupling to the Navier–Stokes equations, see Remark
A.4
.
An exception occurs when
K=L=0
. In this setting, assuming
\alpha\neq 0
,
\beta\neq 0
, and the following compatibility condition on the potentials
F
and
G
,

\displaystyle F(\alpha s)=\alpha\beta G(s)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all~}s\in[-1,1],

the pair
(F^{\prime}(\phi),G^{\prime}(\psi))
then belongs to
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{0,\beta}
, and hence it is an admissible test function in (
A.4
). This choice makes the problematic term containing the normal derivative vanish, which allows us to circumvent the obstruction mentioned above.

4.
Existence of a global-in-time strong solution

In this section, we present the proof of Theorem
3.1
regarding the existence of a global strong solution to (
1.1
).

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Our proof is based on a semi-Galerkin discretization of the Navier–Stokes subsystem combined with Schauder’s fixed point theorem. This method was already used to prove the existence of weak solutions to (
1.1
) in
[
30
]
. In this way, we prove the existence of suitable approximate solutions to (
1.1
). The main part of the proof then relies on establishing higher regularity estimates on these approximate solutions that are uniform with respect to the approximation parameter. Here, we rely on regularity theory for a convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard system with non-degenerate mobility as well as regularity theory for a bulk-surface Stokes system with non-constant coefficients as presented in Appendix
A
and Appendix
B
, respectively. These uniform estimates then allow us to pass to the limit in the corresponding weak formulation of the approximate problem.

Step 1: Definition of the approximate problem.

We consider the bulk-surface Stokes operator (see
[
30
, Section 5.3]
)

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{A}:D(\mathbfcal{A})\subset\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(-\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Omega}(\textnormal{div}(2\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v})),-\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}(\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(2\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+2[\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\,\mathbf{n}]_{\tau}+\mathbf{w})\big),

where
\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Omega}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}
are the Leray projections on
\Omega
and
\Gamma
, respectively, and the domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathbfcal{A})
is given by

\displaystyle D(\mathbfcal{A})=\mathbfcal{H}^{2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}.

It was shown in
[
30
, Theorem 5.9]
that
\mathbfcal{A}
has countably many positive eigenvalues
\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
with corresponding eigenfunctions
\{(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. These eigenfunctions are determined by

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}2\mathbf{D}\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}\int_{\Omega}\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}\int_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
, and can be chosen in such a way that they form an orthonormal basis of
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
. In addition, the eigenfunctions
\{(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
form a complete orthogonal system of
D(\mathbfcal{A})
. For more details, we refer to
[
30
, Section 5.3]
. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we introduce the finite-dimensional subspace

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{V}_{k}\coloneqq\mathrm{span}\,\{(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{1}),\ldots,(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\}\subset\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}},

and denote with
\mathbfcal{P}_{\mathbfcal{V}_{k}}
the
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
-orthogonal projection onto
\mathbfcal{V}_{k}
. Since
\Omega
is of class
C^{3}
, regularity theory for the bulk-surface Stokes operator (see
[
30
, Theorem 5.7]
) yields that
(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j})\in\mathbfcal{W}^{2,r}
for all
r\in[2,\infty)
and all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. As a consequence, the following inverse Sobolev embedding inequalities hold for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{V}_{k}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}}&\leq C_{k}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}},\quad\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{2}}\leq C_{k}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}},\\
\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{W}^{2,r}}&\leq C_{k,r}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}}.\end{split}

(4.1)

Now, fix
T>0
. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we seek an approximate solution
(\mathbf{v}_{k},\mathbf{w}_{k},\phi_{k},\psi_{k},\mu_{k},\theta_{k})
to system (
1.1
) such that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v}_{k},\mathbf{w}_{k})

\displaystyle\in C^{1}([0,T];\mathbfcal{V}_{k}),

(4.2a)

\displaystyle(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{W}^{2,p}),\hskip 10.00002pt(\partial_{t}\phi_{k},\partial_{t}\psi_{k})\in L^{\infty}(0,T;(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1}),

(4.2b)

\displaystyle(\mu_{k},\theta_{k})

\displaystyle\in C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{3})\cap H^{1}(0,T;(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\prime}),

(4.2c)

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}(\phi_{k})

\displaystyle,G^{\prime}(\psi_{k})),\ (F^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{k}),G^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{k}))\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{L}^{p}),

(4.2d)

for any
2\leq p<\infty
, with
|\phi_{k}|<1
a.e. in
Q_{T}
and
|\psi_{k}|<1
a.e. on
\Sigma_{T}
, in the sense that, a.e. on
[0,T]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\rho(\phi_{k})\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{k}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\sigma(\psi_{k})\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Omega}\rho(\phi_{k})(\mathbf{v}_{k}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{k}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\sigma(\psi_{k})(\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\int_{\Omega}m_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})(\nabla\mu_{k}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{k}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\int_{\Gamma}m_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k})(\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

(4.3)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{k}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k})\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{k}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\int_{\Omega}\mu_{k}\nabla\phi_{k}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\theta_{k}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi_{k}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\frac{\chi(L)}{2}\Big(\beta\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}-\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\Big)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\theta_{k}-\mu_{k})\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{V}_{k}
, and

\displaystyle\big<(\partial_{t}\phi_{k},\partial_{t}\psi_{k}),(\zeta,\xi)\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}-\int_{\Omega}\phi_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}\cdot\nabla\zeta\;\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Gamma}\psi_{k}\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

(4.4)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=-\int_{\Omega}m_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})\nabla\mu_{k}\cdot\nabla\zeta\;\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Gamma}m_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k})\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma-\chi(L)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\theta_{k}-\mu_{k})(\beta\xi-\zeta)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

for all
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}
, where

\displaystyle\mu_{k}=-\Delta\phi_{k}+F^{\prime}(\phi_{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{a.e. in~}Q_{T},

(4.5a)

\displaystyle\theta_{k}=-\Delta_{\Gamma}\psi_{k}+G^{\prime}(\psi_{k})+\alpha\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{a.e. on~}\Sigma_{T},

(4.5b)

\displaystyle K\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi_{k}=\alpha\psi_{k}-\phi_{k},\hskip 20.00003ptK\in(0,\infty),

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{a.e. on~}\Sigma_{T},

(4.5c)

together with the initial conditions

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v}_{k},\mathbf{w}_{k})|_{t=0}=\mathbfcal{P}_{\mathbfcal{V}_{k}}(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0}),\hskip 20.00003pt(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})|_{t=0}=(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}\Omega\times\Gamma.

(4.6)

Here, we have used the notation

\displaystyle Q_{T}\coloneqq\Omega\times(0,T),\hskip 20.00003pt\Sigma_{T}\coloneqq\Gamma\times(0,T).

We note that the weak formulation corresponding to (
4.5
) reads as

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mu_{k}\eta\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\theta_{k}\vartheta\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\phi_{k}\cdot\nabla\eta+F^{\prime}(\phi_{k})\eta\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\vartheta+G^{\prime}(\psi_{k})\vartheta\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

(4.7)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\chi(K)\int_{\Gamma}(\alpha\psi_{k}-\phi_{k})(\alpha\vartheta-\eta)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

a.e. on
[0,T]
for all
(\eta,\vartheta)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
.

To show the existence of an approximation solution satisfying (
4.2
)-(
4.6
), we perform a fixed-point argument as in
[
30
]
. Toward this aim, let
(\mathbf{v}_{\ast},\mathbf{w}_{\ast})\in H^{1}(0,T;\mathbfcal{V}_{k})
. We consider the convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi_{k}+\textnormal{div}(\phi_{k}\mathbf{v}_{\ast})=\textnormal{div}(m_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})\nabla\mu_{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q_{T},

(4.8a)

\displaystyle\mu=-\Delta\phi_{k}+F^{\prime}(\phi_{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}Q_{T},

(4.8b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi_{k}+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k}\mathbf{w}_{\ast})=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(m_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k})\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta_{k})-\beta m_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma_{T},

(4.8c)

\displaystyle\theta_{k}=-\Delta_{\Gamma}\psi_{k}+G^{\prime}(\psi_{k})+\alpha\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma_{T},

(4.8d)

\displaystyle K\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi_{k}=\alpha\psi_{k}-\phi_{k}\hskip 20.00003ptK\in(0,\infty),

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma_{T},

(4.8e)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Lm_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu_{k}=\beta\theta_{k}-\mu_{k}&\text{if}\ L\in[0,\infty),\\
m_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu_{k}=0&\text{if}\ L=\infty\end{cases}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Sigma_{T},

(4.8f)

\displaystyle\phi_{k}|_{t=0}=\phi_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in~}\Omega,

(4.8g)

\displaystyle\psi_{k}|_{t=0}=\psi_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on~}\Gamma.

(4.8h)

In view of Theorem
A.3
, there exists a unique strong solution to (
4.8
) such that

\displaystyle(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{3}),

(4.9a)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\phi_{k},\partial_{t}\psi_{k})

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(0,T;(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime})\cap H^{1}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1}),

(4.9b)

\displaystyle(\mu_{k},\theta_{k})

\displaystyle\in C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{3})\cap H^{1}(0,T;(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\prime}),

(4.9c)

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}(\phi_{k})

\displaystyle,G^{\prime}(\psi_{k})),\ (F^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{k}),G^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{k}))\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{L}^{p}),

(4.9d)

for any
2\leq p<\infty
, and it holds

\displaystyle|\phi_{k}|<1\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.e.~in~}Q_{T}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt|\psi_{k}|<1\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.e.~on~}\Sigma_{T}.

(4.10)

Furthermore, the energy inequality (
(iv)
) in combination with (
4.10
) yields that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&E_{\mathrm{free}}(\phi(t),\psi(t))+\frac{\min\{1,m_{\ast}\}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|(\mu,\theta)\|_{L,\beta}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}s\\
&\quad\leq E_{\mathrm{free}}(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})+\frac{1}{2\min\{1,m_{\ast}\}}\int_{0}^{t}\|(\mathbf{v}_{\ast},\mathbf{w}_{\ast})\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}s\end{split}

(4.11)

for all
t\in[0,T]
.
Now, for any
t\in[0,T]
, we make the ansatz

\displaystyle\big(\mathbf{v}_{k}(t),\mathbf{w}_{k}(t)\big)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}^{k}(t)\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{a.e.~in~}\Omega\times\Gamma

as a solution to the Galerkin approximation of (
4.3
) that reads as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}\rho(\phi_{k})\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\sigma(\psi_{k})\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad+\int_{\Omega}\rho(\phi_{k})(\mathbf{v}_{\ast}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\sigma(\psi_{k})(\mathbf{w}_{\ast}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad-\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\int_{\Omega}m_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})(\nabla\mu_{k}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}x-\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\int_{\Gamma}m_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k})(\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad+\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{k}:\mathbf{D}\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k})\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{k}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\quad=\int_{\Omega}\mu_{k}\nabla\phi_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\theta_{k}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad+\frac{\chi(L)}{2}\Big(\beta\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}-\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\Big)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\theta_{k}-\mu_{k})\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,\end{split}

(4.12)

a.e. on
[0,T]
for all
l=1,\ldots,k
, supplemented with the initial condition

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v}_{k},\mathbf{w}_{k})|_{t=0}=\mathbfcal{P}_{\mathbfcal{V}_{k}}(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{a.e. in~}\Omega\times\Gamma.

(4.13)

Setting
\mathbf{A}_{k}(t)=(a_{1}^{k}(t),\ldots,a_{k}^{k}(t))^{\top}
, the system (
4.12
) is equivalent to the system of differential equations

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{k}(t)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbf{A}^{k}(t)+\mathbf{L}^{k}(t)\mathbf{A}^{k}(t)=\mathbf{G}^{k}(t),

(4.14)

where

\displaystyle(\mathbf{M}^{k}(t))_{l,j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\rho(\phi_{k})\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\sigma(\psi_{k})\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{L}^{k}(t))_{l,j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\rho(\phi_{k})(\mathbf{v}_{\ast}\cdot\nabla)\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\sigma(\psi_{k})(\mathbf{w}_{\ast}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\int_{\Omega}m_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})(\nabla\mu_{k}\cdot\nabla)\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\int_{\Gamma}m_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k})(\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})\mathbf{D}\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j}:\mathbf{D}\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k})\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\frac{\chi(L)}{2}\Big(\beta\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}-\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\Big)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\theta_{k}-\mu_{k})\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{G}^{k}(t))_{l}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\mu_{k}\nabla\phi_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\theta_{k}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi_{k}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{l}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

and the initial condition reads as

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{k}(0)_{j}=\big(\mathbfcal{P}_{\mathbfcal{V}_{k}}(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0}),(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j})\big)_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}},\hskip 20.00003ptj=1,\ldots,k.

(4.15)

By (
4.9
) we have
(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})\in C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{2})
, which in turn implies that

\displaystyle\rho(\phi_{k}),\nu_{\Omega}(\phi_{k}),m_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})\in C(\overline{\Omega}\times[0,T])

as well as

\displaystyle\sigma(\psi_{k}),\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k}),m_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k}),\gamma(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})\in C(\Gamma\times[0,T]).

Moreover, as we know that
(\mathbf{v}_{\ast},\mathbf{w}_{\ast})\in C([0,T];\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}})
and
(\mu_{k},\theta_{k})\in C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})
, it follows that
\mathbf{M}^{k},\mathbf{L}^{k}\in C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{k\times k})
and
\mathbf{G}^{k}\in C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Furthermore, the matrix
\mathbf{M}^{k}(\cdot)
is positive definite on
[0,T]
, which entails that
\det\big(\mathbf{M}^{k}(\cdot)\big)
is continuous and uniformly positive on
[0,T]
. Consequently, according to Cramer’s rule for the inverse, we also have
(\mathbf{M}^{k})^{-1}\in C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{k\times k})
. Therefore, the classical existence and uniqueness theorem for systems of linear ODEs entails that there exists a unique vector
\mathbf{A}^{k}\in C^{1}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{k})
solving (
4.14
) together with the initial condition (
4.15
). This shows that there exists a unique solution
(\mathbf{v}_{k},\mathbf{w}_{k})\in C^{1}([0,T];\mathbfcal{V}_{k})
to (
4.12
)-(
4.13
).

Now, we multiply (
4.12
) by
a_{l}^{k}
and sum over
l=1,\ldots,m
to find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\rho(\phi_{k})\partial_{t}|\mathbf{v}_{k}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}\sigma(\psi_{k})\partial_{t}|\mathbf{w}_{k}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\rho(\phi_{k})\mathbf{v}_{\ast}\cdot\nabla|\mathbf{v}_{k}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}\sigma(\psi_{k})\mathbf{w}_{\ast}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}|\mathbf{w}_{k}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi_{k})|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{k}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi_{k})|\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{k}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})|\mathbf{w}_{k}|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma
